<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Pancreatic Pathology: Focus 02-50 (• Infections)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the characteristic histological features and causative agent of acute pancreatitis associated with mumps virus infection. (识别腮腺炎病毒感染相关的急性胰腺炎的特征性组织学表现和致病原)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 22-year-old male college student presents to the emergency department with severe epigastric pain radiating to his back, nausea, and vomiting for the past 24 hours. He reports having had parotitis (swelling of the salivary glands) one week prior, which resolved spontaneously. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he takes no medications. On examination, he is febrile (38.5°C), tachycardic (110 bpm), and has diffuse abdominal tenderness with guarding, particularly in the epigastrium. Serum amylase is markedly elevated (1500 U/L; normal <100 U/L), and lipase is also significantly elevated (800 U/L; normal <160 U/L). An abdominal CT scan shows diffuse pancreatic enlargement with peripancreatic fat stranding. A fine-needle aspiration biopsy of the swollen parotid gland performed during the parotitis episode revealed characteristic multinucleated giant cells. The patient is subsequently treated supportively with intravenous fluids and pain management.

Which of the following histological findings is MOST likely to be observed in a pancreatic biopsy obtained during the acute phase of this patient's illness?

(A) Extensive necrosis with liquefaction and saponification, characteristic of fat necrosis.
(B) Interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltration, and acinar cell degeneration with minimal necrosis.
(C) Formation of large, multinucleated giant cells within the pancreatic parenchyma and ducts.
(D) Dense fibrosis and calcifications within the pancreatic parenchyma, indicative of chronic pancreatitis.
(E) Infiltration by neutrophils and formation of microabscesses, suggestive of bacterial infection.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 22-year-old male college student. This age group is typical for mumps infection.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Severe epigastric pain radiating to the back, nausea, vomiting. Classic symptoms of acute pancreatitis.
- **Recent History:** Parotitis (swelling of salivary glands) one week prior. This is a hallmark symptom of mumps.
- **Physical Exam:** Fever, tachycardia, abdominal tenderness/guarding. Signs consistent with acute inflammation and possible pancreatitis.
- **Lab Findings:** Markedly elevated amylase and lipase. Confirms acute pancreatitis.
- **Imaging:** CT scan showing diffuse pancreatic enlargement and peripancreatic fat stranding. Consistent with acute pancreatitis.
- **Parotid Biopsy Finding:** Multinucleated giant cells. This is a characteristic histological finding in mumps infection.
- **Connecting the Dots:** The patient presents with acute pancreatitis shortly after experiencing symptoms and histological confirmation (multinucleated giant cells) of mumps infection. Mumps virus is a known, albeit uncommon, cause of acute pancreatitis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely histological finding in the pancreas during the acute phase of mumps-associated pancreatitis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Mumps Virus:** An RNA virus belonging to the Paramyxoviridae family. It primarily infects epithelial cells, including those of the salivary glands (causing parotitis) and, less commonly, other organs like the pancreas, testes, ovaries, and meninges.
- **Mumps Pancreatitis:** Occurs when the virus infects pancreatic acinar cells and ductal cells.
- **Histopathology of Mumps Pancreatitis:** The characteristic histological feature is the presence of **multinucleated giant cells** within the pancreatic parenchyma, particularly in the ducts and acini. These giant cells are formed by the fusion of infected epithelial cells, a common cytopathic effect of paramyxoviruses like mumps. There is also associated interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltration, and acinar cell degeneration, but the giant cells are the most distinctive finding. The inflammation is typically mild to moderate, with minimal necrosis compared to other causes like alcohol or gallstones.
- **Mechanism:** Viral infection leads to direct cell damage and inflammation. The immune response also contributes to the pathology.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Extensive necrosis with liquefaction and saponification (fat necrosis) is characteristic of severe acute pancreatitis, often caused by alcohol or gallstones, where massive release of pancreatic enzymes leads to autodigestion of the pancreas and surrounding fat. While some necrosis can occur in mumps pancreatitis, it's typically not the predominant feature, and liquefaction/saponification are more associated with severe forms of other etiologies. Mumps pancreatitis is usually milder.
- (B): Interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltration, and acinar cell degeneration are present in mumps pancreatitis, but they are non-specific findings seen in many types of pancreatitis, including interstitial pancreatitis. This option lacks the specific hallmark feature of mumps infection.
- (D): Dense fibrosis and calcifications are characteristic features of **chronic** pancreatitis, not acute pancreatitis. This patient presents with acute symptoms and findings.
- (E): Infiltration by neutrophils and formation of microabscesses suggest a **bacterial** infection of the pancreas (suppurative pancreatitis). While bacterial infection can complicate pancreatitis, the clinical history (recent mumps) and the specific finding of multinucleated giant cells in the parotid strongly point towards a viral etiology (mumps), which does not typically cause suppurative pancreatitis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Mumps Pancreatitis (Viral)                                  | Severe Alcoholic/Gallstone Pancreatitis (Enzymatic) | Chronic Pancreatitis (Fibrotic) | Bacterial Pancreatitis (Suppurative) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------------------- |
| **Etiology**            | Mumps virus infection                                       | Alcohol, gallstones                                 | Chronic alcohol abuse, etc.    | Bacterial infection (e.g., E. coli) |
| **Histology**           | Multinucleated giant cells, edema, lymphocytic infiltrate | Extensive necrosis, fat necrosis, saponification   | Fibrosis, calcifications      | Neutrophils, microabscesses        |
| **Clinical Course**     | Usually mild to moderate, self-limited                     | Often severe, potentially life-threatening          | Progressive, chronic pain      | Often severe, may require drainage |
| **Key Pathognomonic Clue** | Multinucleated giant cells                                 | Fat necrosis, saponification                        | Calcifications                | Neutrophilic infiltrate, abscesses  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Mumps virus can cause acute pancreatitis, typically presenting after parotitis. The characteristic histological finding is the presence of multinucleated giant cells in the pancreatic parenchyma, reflecting the viral cytopathic effect. Differentiate this from other causes of acute pancreatitis (e.g., alcohol, gallstones) which often show more severe necrosis and fat necrosis, and from chronic pancreatitis (fibrosis, calcifications) and bacterial pancreatitis (neutrophils, abscesses).
